The Working Group was represented by\nMr. Matthew Gillett (New Zealand), Ms. Ganna Yudkivska (Ukraine), and Ms. Miriam Estrada-Castillo\n(Ecuador), and accompanied by staff of the Office of the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights.\nThe Working Group first visited Mexico in 2002, approximately 20 years before this second official visit of\n2023. The Working Group would like to express its gratitude to the Government of Mexico for its invitation\nto undertake the country visit and for its cooperation. During the visit, the Working Group met with the\nofficials of the following Federal authorities: Ministry of the Interior, Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Ministry of\nHealth, Ministry of Security and Citizens’ Protection, Ministry of Defense, Ministry of the Navy, Attorney-\nGeneral’s Office, Supreme Court, Council of the Federal Judiciary, Federal Institute of Public Defender, the\nCongress of the Union, the Decentralized administrative body for the prevention and social rehabilitation, the\nNational Migration Institute, the National System for the Protection of Children and Adolescents, the National\nCommission to Prevent and Eradicate Violence against Women, the National Institute for Indigenous People,\nthe Commission for Dialogue with Indigenous Peoples and National Human Rights Commission, as well as\nauthorities in the states of Mexico City, Nuevo León and Chiapas.\nThe Working Group would like to thank the OHCHR Mexico Office and their staff as well as the UN Country\nTeam and the Resident Coordinator for supporting the visit. The Working Group also recognizes the numerous\nstakeholders from civil society and interviewees who shared their perspectives on the arbitrary deprivation of\nliberty. The Group thanks all of them for the information and assistance they provided.\nThese observations constitute the Working Group’s preliminary findings from its country visit. They will\nserve as a basis for future deliberations between the members of the Working Group at its forthcoming sessions\nin Geneva. The Working Group will submit its report to the UN Human Rights Council in September 2024.\nDuring the country visit, the Working Group enjoyed full and unimpeded access and went to 4 federal facilities\nand 11 State and municipal facilities. These included penitentiaries (both men’s and women’s at the federal\nand state level), police stations, migration detention centres, adolescent centres, child protection shelters,\nmental health hospitals and prosecutors’ offices. It confidentially interviewed around 173 persons deprived of\ntheir liberty. The Working Group received unimpeded access to all places it sought to visit and it expresses it\nappreciation to the Federal and States Governments for their cooperation throughout the country visit.\n\n                                 1. Good practices and positive developments\nThe Working Group welcomes the constitutional, legal and regulatory reforms, introduced in Mexico since\nits 2002 visit. After the Working Group’s first visit, Mexico has ratified several international human rights\ntreaties, including on the International Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, the Inter-\nAmerican Convention on the Protection of human Rights of Older Persons, and the Optional Protocol to the\nUN Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment (OPCAT)\non 11 April 2005. In 2007, the National Preventive Mechanism against Torture was created a as part of the\nNational Human Rights Commission. The system of regular, independent monitoring of all places of\ndeprivation of liberty that OPCAT puts in place is not only a crucial tool for the prevention of torture and ill-\ntreatment, but also for combating the arbitrary deprivation of liberty. Equally, the Working Group commends\nMexico’s 2008 ratification of the International Convention for the Protection of All Persons from Enforced\nDisappearance and, since 2020, the acceptance of the individual communications procedure. The Working\nGroup notes that enforced disappearances constitute an aggravated form of arbitrary detention. Additionally,\nthe Working Group notes the following positive developments to prevent the occurrence of arbitrary detention.\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                1\n\n1.1 Amendments to consolidate a human rights-centred and accusatory model of criminal justice\nIn 2008, Mexico transitioned to an accusatory model of criminal justice, which led to a significant reduction\nof practices that violated human rights. The presumption of innocence, now prescribed as a foundational\nprinciple, is instrumental for the avoidance of arbitrary detention. The new framework also provides that\ndefendants have the right to appear before a judge and has reinforced victims’ rights. Constitutional\nAmendments in 2011 placed human rights front and centre in the Constitution. The Working Group also notes\nimprovements introduced pursuant to the 2016 Law on the Execution of Penalties and commends the increased\nuse of conciliation boards to facilitate early release, for example in Chiapas. However, the Working Group\nemphasizes that Mexico must bring its Constitution and laws into conformity with all of its international\nhuman rights obligations, as detailed in these findings.1\n1.2 Introduction of a National Registry of Detentions\nIn 2019, Mexico introduced a constitutional reform which established that the authorities must maintain a\nNational Registry of Detentions. This accords with the Working Group’s previous recommendation that a\nproper detention registration system is essential for preventing arbitrary detention.2 The Ministry of Security\nand Citizen’s Protection oversees the National Registry of Detentions. Almost 2,000,000 searches of the\ndatabase have been made by members of the public, and around 30 percent of those searches revealed that the\nperson was in detention. Detentions must be entered into the registry within 5 hours of deprivation of liberty.\nNonetheless, the National Registry does not capture all detentions. In particular, administrative detentions of\nmigrants and persons with psycho-social disabilities are not included. This creates an information gap which\nreduces the authorities’ ability to prevent and redress arbitrary detention of migrants and other vulnerable\npersons. Moreover, whereas the Supreme Court held that military personnel, including those incorporated into\nthe National Guard, must enter detentions into the Registry, several exceptions to this requirement persist. The\nWorking Group emphasises that authorities must ensure that all officials carrying detentions of any nature,\nincluding those in the Army, Navy and National Guard, are required to enter those detentions into the National\nRegister and that all officials, civilian and military, are aware of this responsibility.\n1.3 Restriction of administration of justice by military courts\nIn its 2002 country visit report on Mexico, the Working Group cautioned that military courts should not be\nused to adjudicate military members accused of crimes ordinarily dealt with by civilian courts, such as drug-\ntrafficking and enforced disappearance. In 2014, Mexico amended its laws to require that military members\nwho commit crimes against civilians are tried before civilian courts. The Working Group considers this is a\npositive means of reducing the risk of impunity among military members for crimes including enforced\ndisappearance and arbitrary detention. However, it notes that all human rights violations by military personnel\nshould fall under the civilian jurisdiction.\n1.4 National and State Human Rights Commissions and non-governmental human rights organizations\nThe Working Group met with the National Human Rights Commission and some state human rights bodies,\nas well as an array of non-governmental organizations which also conduct human rights work. It recalls that\nNational Human Rights Commission and the state-level human rights commissions must perform their\nfunctions in an autonomous and independent manner.\nThe Working Group welcomes the fact that the National Human Rights Commission has a legal basis under\nArticle 102 of the Constitution, in accordance with the Paris Principles on National Human Rights\nMechanisms. However, the Working Group was concerned to learn that visits to persons deprived of their\nliberty by members of the national and state human rights commissions did not involve follow-up to inform\nthem of efforts to redress their complaints.\n\n\n\n1 Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties, Article 27.\n2 Report of the Working Group on Arbitrary Detention for 2007, A/HRC/7/4, para. 69. See also Principle 12 of the Body of Principles for the\n\nProtection of All Persons under Any Form of Detention or Imprisonment.\n                                                                                                                                              2\n\nWith respect to non-governmental organizations, the Working Group was concerned to hear of specific\ninstances in which human rights defenders were subjected to reprisals, including through threats to fabricate\ncharges against them. These included members of civil society advocating for the rights of members of\nminorities such as the LGBTI+ community, indigenous persons, and afro-Mexicans. Such reprisals violate\ninter alia Article 12 of the Declaration on Human Rights Defenders.3\n\n                                               2. Main Findings\nThe Working Group notes that arbitrary detention remains a widespread problem in Mexico due to a variety\nof causes and aggravating factors, both in the regulatory framework and in its implementation.\n\n2.1 Criminal Justice\n2.1.1. Mandatory Pre-trial Detention\nAlthough pre-trial detention should be regarded as an exceptional measure to ensure the availability of the\naccused during criminal proceedings, Article 19 of the Mexican Constitution sets out a list of felonies that\ntrigger mandatory pre-trial detention. In 2019, mandatory pre-trial detention was extended to 16 categories of\ncrimes, which are vaguely framed and susceptible to extension to an indeterminate range of offences. In 2021,\nmandatory pre-trial detention was introduced for other crimes.\nOf the approximately 90,000 people in pre-trial detention (“en proceso”) in 2022, around 50 percent are subject\nto mandatory pre-trial detention. Many of the detainees had been subjected to lengthy mandatory pre-trial\ndetention, including some who were still in mandatory pre-trial detention over 5 years after arrest. Worryingly,\nexcessively lengthy pre-trial detention was common amongst all detainees (noting that there was a total prison\npopulation of around 230,000 in Mexico in July 2023), not just those in mandatory pre-trial detention.\nThe Working Group is mindful that mandatory pre-trial detention has been identified as a violation of the right\nto personal liberty, as well as potentially undermining others, such as the presumption of innocence, the right\nto personal integrity and judicial independence. Mandatory pre-trial detention has been considered a violation\nof human rights in several opinions of the Working Group, including the Mexican context,4 and was declared\nto be a violation of Mexico’s obligations by the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, which ordered it to\nadjust its laws in this respect to meet international standards.\nThe Mexican authorities should reconcile its approach to preventive detention with international human rights\nlaw, by removing mandatory pre-trial detention from the Constitution and requiring that pre-trial detention\nmay only be applied based on an individualized assessment in which it is shown that the defendant is either a\nflight risk, at risk of serious re-offending, or at risk of tampering with evidence or witnesses.\n2.1.2. Arraigo (precautionary detention without charge)\nThe procedure known as arraigo was incorporated into the Constitution in 2008. It permits the authorities to\ndetain a person for up to 80 days for criminal investigation purposes in organized crime, without indicting that\nperson for a crime. The use of arraigo peaked between 2009 and 2012, but has decreased since 2013, and is\nrarely used these days. According to the information received by the Working Group, in 2022 it was used in\n25 cases. An attempt to remove arraigo from the Constitution in 2019 was unsuccessful.\nThe Inter-American Court of Human Rights and other international bodies have called for Mexico to eliminate\narraigo, as it violates the right to personal liberty and due process. Although authorities explained that arraigo\nis currently mainly used to protect witnesses in organised crimes cases, the Working Group notes that its\nongoing existence creates the risk of arbitrary detention. It encourages the Mexican authorities to remove the\nprocedure of arraigo from the Constitution and end its use as a basis for detention.\n\n\n\n\n3 UNGA, A/RES/53/144 8 March 1999 (“Declaration on the Right and Responsibility of Individuals, Groups and Organs of Society to Promote\n\nand Protect Universally Recognized Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms”).\n4 See, e.g. Opinion Nos. 32/2023; 14/2021; 1/2018.\n\n                                                                                                                                          3\n\n2.1.3. The militarization of public security\nIn Mexico, the use of military forces to undertake public policing activities at the federal, state and municipal\nlevel is frequent. There are reportedly over one hundred thousand soldiers currently carrying out policing and\npublic security tasks, as well as migratory control. This has been closely linked to rises in violence against\ndetainees, disappearances, and homicides. Detainees arrested by military personnel frequently were subjected\nto serious violence and torture, including beatings with foreign objects.\nSymptomatic of the extensive use of military elements in policing is the 2019 formation of the National Guard.\nAccording to the Constitution, the National Guard falls under the Ministry of Security and Citizen’s Protection.\nAlthough the incorporation of military personnel into the National Guard has a transitory nature, the armed\nforces have been authorised to continue public security functions until 2028.\nWhereas the National Guard took over the functions of the Federal Police, its personnel are predominantly\nmilitary by background. Of its 128,000 members, around 48 percent come from the Army and Navy. Although\nmembers of the National Guard are trained inter alia in human rights and in gathering evidence, the Working\nGroup notes that there are ongoing reports of violence in arrests and arbitrary detention by military personnel.\nAlthough arbitrary arrests committed by personnel of the Ministry of Defence and Navy have reportedly\ndecreased, 60 of 240 complaints of arbitrary detention from 2018-2023 remain unresolved.\n2.1.4. Excessive use of force during arrests\nDuring its country visit, the Working Group learned that a significant number of detainees suffer serious and\nexcessive violence during their arrests, such as a detainee being shot in the stomach by police, resulting parts\nof his intestine being removed. International human rights mechanisms, including the Working Group in its\nopinions, have also referred to ill-treatment and torture committed by security forces in Mexico.\nOften beatings and torture are inflicted to extract confessions. Detainees are sometimes forced to give\ninformation about other alleged suspects or potential evidence and ill-treatment is also used as a matter of\nhumiliation and punishment. For example, indigenous persons are sometimes beaten to force them to accept\nresponsibility and called “indio” during arrests. A woman who was pregnant at the time of arrest and informed\nofficers of this was hit in the stomach area and then driven around for hours by police to various locations to\nlook for evidence to incriminate her and her boyfriend. Others were “taken for a drive”, during which officers\nor military members did not deliver them promptly to the nearest civil authority, prosecutor, or judge, but\ninstead took them to remote locations and inflicted violence against them with a view to forcing them to\nprovide incriminating evidence.\nThese risks are exacerbated by the fact that a violative practice has emerged whereby the 48 hours within\nwhich a detainee must be brought before a judge is only counted beginning from when the detainee is presented\nto the prosecutor. This means that the time spent in police (or other security force) custody is not included in\nthe counting of the 48 hours, as was also accepted by authorities. Given that the initial period after arrest is a\ncritical time when a detainee is in most jeopardy of unduly self-incriminating or otherwise prejudicing their\nrights, it is critical that the 48 hours is counted from the time of the initial physical deprivation of liberty, rather\nthan from some later point in time. The Working Group also notes with concern that the 48 hr period is\nfrequently doubled to 96 hours in cases of organized crime. Moreover, the Working Group found deficiencies\nin the recording of apprehensions, with a variety of books, spreadsheets and other lists used across various\ninstitutions without a consistent methodology. Regarding accountability, although the authorities did refer to\ninstances of security agents being investigated for violence against detainees, they did not provide details of\nany significant number of convictions for such activities.\n2.1.5. Abuse of flagrante delicto\nMany arrests in Mexico are carried out without a pre-existing warrant, under the justification of flagrante\ndelicto. Since 2016, the rules governing the accusatory criminal process require that people detained in\nflagrante delicto without an arrest warrant must be brought before a judge to carry out a control of the\ndetention. However, the Working Group notes that expanded interpretations of flagrante delicto continue to\n\n                                                                                                                      4\n\nbe used. These arrests under expanded interpretations violate human rights, as set out in several opinions by\nthe Group on Arbitrary Detention.\nThe 2021 national survey of persons deprived of liberty indicated that 23 percent of detainees were arrested\nin the streets without an arrest warrant and that over 40 percent claimed that they were falsely accused of\ncommitting a crime. The Working Group recalls that, where a warrant is not obtained in advance, the scope of\njudicial control over detention is restricted to an ex post facto measure, by which time a violation may have\noccurred. There are circumstances, such as flagrante delicto or being \"caught in the act\", in which there is no\nopportunity to obtain a warrant ex ante. However, these must be interpreted restrictively to avoid undermining\njudicial control of detention.\nIn this respect, the Working Group was concerned to learn that an extended version of flagrante delicto, known\nas flagrante por señalamiento, or flagrancy by report, is applied in Mexico, whereby a person can be detained\nwithout a warrant at a later point in time despite not having been caught in the act by an official with policing\npowers, so long as a witness, who may be a private person, reports having seen them committing a crime.\nAnother extended application of this notion relates to the situation when a person who allegedly committed a\ncrime is not arrested immediately but is purportedly followed for a certain amount of time via street cameras,\nand then only arrested at a later stage. These extended interpretations of flagrante delicto are not consistent\nwith human rights law and must be prohibited.\n2.1.6. Deprivation of liberty under drug policy\nFrom 2006, Mexico has taken a far more punitive approach towards the use and dealing of drugs. The\npossession of drugs is still criminalised and even possession at relatively low levels can be sufficient to meet\nthe threshold to qualify as dealing drugs. As a result, there has been an increase in the imprisonment of drug\nusers. The Working Group emphasises the importance of looking for alternatives to detention in this context.\nConcerningly, the Working Group was informed of instances in which security forces fabricated charges\nagainst persons by placing drugs in their homes, vehicles, bags, or clothes. Reportedly, this practice continues\nto be rife among security forces. Allegations of such manufacturing of crimes must be taken extremely\nseriously, thoroughly investigated, and appropriately sanctioned where established. Where security forces can\nbe equipped with body-cams or other recording devices, this can assist to a degree in reducing the possibility\nfor such abuses of power. Additionally, the Working Group learned that detainees are often pre-textually\ncharged for specific offences only to have them changed to different crimes once they are detained, which can\nundermine their rights to liberty, against self-incrimination and to an effective defence.\nThe Amnesty Law includes crimes related to drug offending, provided that they fall under federal jurisdiction.\nIt covers vulnerable persons such as those in extreme poverty or indigenous people. However, the Working\nGroup was told that only around 320 people had been released via amnesty, as the parameters of the law have\nbeen restrictively interpreted and the Amnesty Committee has only met five times in three years. The Working\nGroup recalls that detaining a person despite there being an amnesty law applicable to them constitutes\narbitrary detention under Category I. It strongly encourages the Amnesty Committee to avoid an overly\nrestrictive approach to Amnesty Law, and to ensure that it implements amnesty-based releases expeditiously.\nOn a related note, the Working Group observed that photos of detainees posed in front of tables displaying\ndrugs and weapons often appear in the media, sometimes with the names of detainees or other identifying\ninformation. Such images have the potential to undermine the presumption of innocence in any subsequent\ncriminal proceedings. Although authorities have reportedly prohibited their own forces from disseminating\nsuch images, the posing of arrested detainees and illicit materials for the purpose of taking photos cannot be\ndone without at least the complicity of the arresting forces. Whilst media freedom must be respected, the\nWorking Group implores the Government of Mexico to ensure that its forces are not complicit in disseminating\nsuch images to the public prior to or during the criminal trials of any such detainees.\n2.1.7. Abbreviated trial procedure\nSince 2016, an abbreviated trial procedure has been incorporated into Mexican legislation by the reform of\nthe criminal process. Although the purpose is to reduce the strain on the criminal justice system, the Working\n                                                                                                               5\n\nGroup learned that detainees were pressured, often by their own lawyers (frequently public defenders), to\naccept responsibility in order to avoid a trial and possible greater sanction. Whereas it should always be open\nto guilty persons to acknowledge culpability, and whereas this can expedite proceedings and avoid re-\ntraumatizing victims, caution must be taken to ensure that such abbreviated procedures are not used as a means\nof dissuading detainees from exercising their legitimate fair trial rights, including the right to silence, the\npresumption of innocence, and the right to adequate time and facilities to conduct their defence.\n2.1.8. Bureaucratisation of the judicial appeal (amparo trial-habeas corpus)\nThe procedure known as amparo, a judicial remedy available in Mexico, is similar to a habeas corpus writ. It\nis provided for in the Constitution and the Amparo Act of 2013. This writ can protect human rights, including\nto liberty and life. Under Mexican law, the writ of amparo is federal remedy, irrespective of whether a case\nconcerns crimes falling under a State entity’s jurisdiction (“común” crimes).\nWhile this procedure allows for release, the Working Group found out that this rarely occurs. For example, in\nCDMX, only 16.5% of cases in which arbitrary detention is found to have occurred result in the immediate\nrelease of the detainee. Similarly, the Committee on Enforced Disappearance noted, as a result of its country\nvisit in 2021, that few judges make use the amparo procedure (“amparo buscador”).5 As a matter of procedure,\nthe Working Group was assured that judges can order additional information from the prosecution office or\nother authorities in order to address amparo requests. However, of the detainees who exercised the right, the\nvast majority had seen their amparo rejected without any further inquiry. Others had seen their amparo requests\nremain undecided for years, including one case in which an individual had remained in detention for 9 years\nwithout trial. Of the few who had successful amparo proceedings, they had nonetheless not been immediately\nreleased but some remained in detention awaiting a re-trial or reactivation of charges which had been held in\nabeyance while they served their existing sentence.\nThe Working Group considers that the amparo procedure has considerable potential to ensure that human\nrights are respected and that individuals are released where violations have occurred. However, the Mexican\njudiciary must ensure that judges vigorously exercise their powers under the amparo procedure. For its part,\nthe Government and the judicial branch must ensure that all judges are aware of their powers to permit release\npursuant to amparo requests, and that they are not retaliated against for doing so.\n2.1.9. Judicial independence\nAn independent and impartial judiciary is essential to combatting arbitrary detention, as reflected inter alia by\nArticle 14(1) of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights. The Working Group welcomes\npositive changes in this respect introduced with the reform of the criminal justice system. It was however\nconcerned to learn that members of the executive authorities have pressured judges, particularly when those\njudges have ruled that executive initiatives contravene the law. In some cases, executive members name\nindividual judges who issued decisions or opinions impugning government policies. More broadly, the federal\njudiciary is facing scathing budgetary cuts, reportedly up to 20 percent. Such measures undermine the ability\nof the judiciary to independently conduct its work without fear nor favour. The Working Group is concerned\nthat this has contributed to the ongoing persistence of high numbers of cases of arbitrary detention at both the\nfederal and state levels.\n2.1.10 Right to legal assistance\nThe Working Group welcomes the information that approximately 95 percent of individuals charged with\nfelonies are assigned public defenders to represent them free of charge. It also appreciates the efforts to provide\na wide range of important legal services, including legal assistance to migrants. Additionally, it notes that the\ntransition to an accusatory system has placed public defence lawyers on a more equal footing with the\nprosecution than previously. However, public defenders face heavy caseloads which compromise the quality\nof their services. Many detainees receive poor quality legal services. Moreover, the caseload results in a\nsignificant burden for the Public Defender’s Offices (PDOs), which has financial and staffing constraints.\n\n\n5 Para.59.\n\n                                                                                                                 6\n\nThe result is a heightened risk of arbitrary detention particularly for persons lacking socio-economic means to\nobtain private legal assistance. Additionally, many detainees lack regular access to public defence counsel\nonce detained; they are unable to contact lawyers by phone, as reportedly such phone calls are often not\nfacilitated free of charge and public defenders often ignore calls from a detention facility. The Working Group\nreiterates its call to strengthen the PDOs (building on its recommendation (d) of its 2002 country visit report).\nIt calls on the authorities to improve mechanisms for access to legal assistance (including through public\ndefenders), particularly for detained persons, in a regular and timely manner.\n\n2.2 Protections in detention\n2.2.1. Conditions of detention\nSafeguards for detained persons help to prevent arbitrary detention. Under human rights law, safeguards\ninclude facilitating prompt and continuing access to a defence lawyer, conducting a post-arrest medical\nexamination, and communications with third parties as well as visits by external oversight mechanisms.\nThe Working Group learned that these safeguards vary from one facility to another and are frequently not fully\nobserved by the authorities in Mexico. Detainees struggled to obtain access to a lawyer once detained. Many\ndetainees were only allowed one ten-minute phone call per 8 days, which they could use either to talk to their\nfamily or lawyer. Given that they usually choose to talk to family and given that lawyers rarely visit the\ndetention facilities which are often located in remote areas, this restriction has considerable potential to\nprejudice pre-trial detainees' exercise of fair trial rights.\nIll-treatment and violence during detention can also impact on a detainee’s ability to exercise their rights,\nparticularly if they suffer injuries as a result thereof. At the federal level, many detainees suffered excessive\nviolence during their arrests but that the Working Group heard that penitentiary staff generally did not use\nviolence against them. However, there were instances of violence by prison guards, indicating that this\nviolative practice has not been eliminated. There is also corruption in some facilities. Moreover, pre-trial\ndetainees (not subject to any conviction) were often housed together with convicted detainees. Several\ndetainees had serious wounds and illnesses and complained that they were unable to access effective medical\ntreatment. A particularly prevalent concern was the lack of medication available to unwell detainees. Many\ncould only get medication if their families obtained it for them. The Working Group emphasizes that where it\nis medically necessary, the authorities must ensure its availability to detainees. Detainees also complained\nabout improper food and the Working Group observed overcrowded conditions in some facilities, with\ndetainees sometimes sleeping on concrete floors without blankets being provided, which are not compatible\nwith human rights protections.\nIn some prisons at the federal and state levels, detainees are kept locked in their cells most of the day and\nrarely, if ever, leave the cell block in which they were housed. Prisons are hampered by chronic staff shortages,\nincluding in key roles such as security guards and prison medical staff. Those shortages impact on their ability\nto take detainees out of their cell blocks for exercise and activities. This was particularly noticeable at the\nmen's federal facilities visited, where many detainees had spent months without leaving their closed-in cell\nblocks.\nRegarding sanctions in detention, the Working Group noted that detainees found to violate prison rules were\noften placed in deplorable cells, with no bedding and only hard concrete floors to sleep on. Such conditions\nare not compatible with human rights guarantees as reflected in the Nelson Mandela Rules. Moreover, solitary\nconfinement was available for use as a punishment for up to 15 days for misconduct. In reality, however, the\nWorking Group found out that confinement for 30 days and more continues, albeit usually in small groups.\nThe Working Group recalls that Rule 45 of the Nelson Mandela Rules provides that solitary confinement shall\nbe used only in exceptional cases as a last resort, for as short a time as possible and subject to independent\nreview, and only pursuant to the authorization by a competent authority. It should be prohibited in the case of\nprisoners with psycho-social disabilities or physical disabilities.\nA systemic safeguard for detainees is prison registries. However, the Working Group found that registries in\nprisons were non-effective, as they were not consistently utilised and updated by the authorities. There were\n                                                                                                               7\n\nconcerns raised about poor internet connectivity, old equipment, and a lack of trained human resources to\nupdate the registry. These shortcomings must be addressed to ensure this tool is available.\nWhile some prisons were under capacity, the Working Group also discovered that severe over-crowding in\nsome male prisons visited by the Group is of particular concern. In one of them, for example, 16 pre-trial\ndetainees are kept locked in small rooms. The Working Group calls upon the authorities to set minimum\nrequirements in respect of the floor space and cubic content of air, per inmate to respect human rights including\nthe right to adequate time and facilities to prepare a defence. It also reiterates the link between overcrowding\nand an excessive length of pre-trial detention, which has been found by this Group to violate the ICCPR, and\nencourages the authorities to resort to alternative preventive measures.\n\n2.3 People in vulnerable situations\n2.3.1. Migrants\nThe Working Group notes that Mexico is a transitory step for migrants seeking to access the United States of\nAmerica from Central and South America as well as a growing array of other states. Many of those people are\nasylum seekers. In recent years, the situation in Central America, Ecuador, and Venezuela has led to a dramatic\nincrease in the numbers of migrants passing through Mexico, or in some cases using it as destination country.\nFlamed by the migration policies of the United States of America, the situation has resulted in migrants often\nbeing subjected to conditions in contravention of Mexico's human rights commitments.\nLarge numbers of migrants and asylum seekers are detained in Mexico, amounting to over 240,000 in the first\nhalf of 2023. Under the Constitution, such administrative detentions should be limited to 36 hours and the\nSupreme Court has held that detention of migrants for 15-60 days under the Migration Law is unconstitutional.\nHowever, the Working Group discovered that a significant number of detained migrants are held beyond the\n36-hour time limit, which raises the risk of arbitrary detention. Moreover, the Working Group learned that\nwhen migrants who are detained for crimes seek to exercise legal rights, such as amparo, then they are often\ntold they will be kept for months instead of being let go in 36 hours. There should be no punishment for\nexercising legal rights, particularly in context of migration.\nA large number of children are detained in the context of migration. In 2022 alone, over 126,000 were\nchannelled into centres run by the National Agency for Family Development (“DIF”). Under Article 99 of the\nMigration Act, children are not permitted to be held in migrant detention. However, in practice the Working\nGroup found that children migrants were typically deprived of liberty, either in shelters solely for\nunaccompanied minors run by DIF or in facilities co-located with migrant facilities under the authority of the\nInstitute for Migration of the Ministry of the Interior.\nHaving visited children deprived of liberty under the authority of the DIF, the Working Group noted that,\nalthough they were fed and sheltered, it is important that the Mexican authorities provide them with sufficient\nopportunities to ensure their development is not harmed and to ensure that any such detention is exceptional,\nwhen alternatives to detention are manifestly unfeasible, and that any such detention is limited in time.\nHaving also visited children deprived of liberty with family members in facilities co-located with migration\ndetention facilities, the Working Group was concerned at the conditions. Children and their family members\nwere sleeping in courtyard areas outside the facility buildings, in the heat. Moreover, whilst law reforms had\nreportedly prohibited the detention of child migrants, the Working Group saw children who were manifestly\nbelow 18 (and in some cases clearly below 10 years of age) deprived of liberty in these areas co-located with\nmigration detention facilities.\nInside the migrant detention facility buildings themselves, the detained persons were not free to circulate\nwithin the facilities. Concerningly, the Working Group saw that metal gates with locks separated the rooms\nwhere migrants were housed from exits to the outside courtyards and guards prevented migrants from freely\naccessing those open-air locations. This also prevented migrants from accessing the complaints boxes of the\nrelevant human rights commissions. Given the fire at the Ciudad Juárez facilities in March 2023, in which 40\n\n\n                                                                                                               8\n\npeople died and many were injured, the Working Group emphasizes that migrants should not be kept behind\nlocked doors and should have access to outdoor areas freely.\nMigrants also are subjected to frequent extortion of bribes by Mexican officials, often from the security forces.\nThose unable to pay were consequently detained, whereas others who were able to pay were allowed to go on\ntheir way. The widespread occurrence of such extortion risks creating an underground economy, which may\nbecome entrenched if security forces grow accustomed to extracting additional income in this unlawful\nmanner. Any such extortion must be prohibited and stamped out in law and in practice.\n2.3.2 Adolescents\nThe detention of adolescents from age 12 up to 17 in Mexico is subject to a special legal regime. Adolescents\nwho are detained are kept in separate facilities from adults. While the facilities were largely clean and the\nWorking Group did not learn of violence by staff or any other shortcomings within the course of the\nproceedings against them or in the detention, opportunities for external activities, such as visits to sporting\nand cultural events, were relatively limited and only available to small groups among the youths in the\nfacilities. Given the developmental stage of their lives, it is important that all the detained youths should have\nopportunities to participate in stimulating activities.\nMore concerningly, many adolescents experience considerable violence in their initial arrests and are not\nnotified of the reasons for the arrest. This is reflected in a national survey which revealed that 66 percent of\nyouths complained of suffering some kind of violent attack during their arrests. Youths were sometimes beaten\nby arresting forces who placed bags over their heads, and taken for drives during which they were pressured\nto confess to crimes. Moreover, adolescents are sometimes told by the defence lawyers to plead guilty to the\ncrimes in order to avoid going into the adult criminal justice system. The Working Group emphasizes the need\nfor adolescents to be given access to appropriately trained lawyers who rigorously defend their interests, given\nthe vulnerable age they are at and the detrimental impact that being incarcerated will typically have on their\nfuture lives and physical and psychological well-being.\n2.3.3 Indigenous people\nThe Working Group learned of instances in which indigenous people faced discrimination leading to their\narrest and subjection to considerable violence by security forces. These included cases where the indigenous\npersons were advocating for the rights of their people, including on governmental premises and projects, and\nwere charged with trespass and criminal damage. In this respect, the Working Group emphasizes that persons\nshould not be subjected to detention merely due to exercising their rights, and that this is particularly important\nfor indigenous groups engaging in non-violent protest actions. The Working Group notes that many indigenous\npersons advocate to protect the environment, and thus have a dual-faceted role, seeking to uphold the rights\nof their fellow indigenous people while also defending nature. Such activities should not be met by criminal\nsanction unless they involve serious violent offending or other grave crimes which cannot be addressed\nthrough alternative means. At the same time, if indigenous groups dispense traditional justice involving\ndetention, this must accord with Mexico’s human rights obligations including the prohibition of arbitrary\ndetention and the prohibition of torture, cruel, inhuman, and degrading treatment.\nFor indigenous persons in detention, which constitutes a significant proportion of detainees particularly in\nChiapas, the Working Group learned that culturally appropriate food was not consistently available and that\nthey were not consistently able to observe traditional practices. Regarding language, the Working Group notes\nthe efforts of the National Institute of Indigenous Peoples, which assisted over 500,000 people in 2022 and an\nincreasing number in 2023. This is important from the outset of deprivation of liberty, when the jeopardy of\nundermining legal rights is highest. The Working Group encourages the authorities to be proactive in training\nprosecutors, judges and other officials in the needs of indigenous persons in detention, which extend beyond\nlanguage and also encompass socio-economic challenges, remoteness, and alienation from their peoples.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                 9\n\n2.3.4 Detention of persons with psycho-social disabilities\nAccording to the Ministry of Health, approximately 24 million persons in Mexico have psycho-social\ndisabilities, 3.5 million of which have chronic symptoms that seriously impact their lives. Many of these\npersons face detention. The Working Group emphasizes that persons with psycho-social disabilities should be\naddressed by the authorities under the framework of health treatment and consistently with the right to liberty.6\nUntil recently, individuals deemed incompetent to participate in criminal proceedings were nonetheless able\nto be sanctioned for crimes through detention for a period corresponding to the crime. In 2019, the General\nHealth Law was amended to introduce a new model for the treatment of those with psychiatric and/or\nsubstance abuse disorders and place more emphasis on the patients’ autonomy and freedom of choice of\ntreatment. If individuals are diagnosed as having psycho-social disabilities that preclude their fitness to stand\ntrial for a crime, they can be detained via an involuntary internment as a safety measure, and the period of\ninternment relates to the disorder of the individual rather than the nature of the crime committed.\nDespite these changes, the Working Group was concerned to discover that individuals with serious psycho-\nsocial disabilities detained in penitentiaries, along with persons convicted for serious crimes. Although those\nwith disorders were sometimes housed in separate blocks, they were otherwise subject to the same carceral\nconditions as the general population. Only negligible psycho-social disabilities treatment was available, with\nno psychiatrists among the prisons’ medical staff on site. It also emerged that persons deemed incapable of\nparticipating in trial proceedings are detained at prison facilities in some cases. The Working Group\nemphasizes that persons with serious psycho-social disabilities which preclude their competence to be tried\nunder criminal law should never be detained at criminal penitentiaries. Moreover, any person detained,\nincluding persons detained under the “safe-guarding” mechanism for their personal health, should be\nsystematically recorded in the National Register of Detentions, with appropriate care paid to protect their\nprivate personal data and health-related information.\n2.3.5 LGBTI+ persons\nWith respect to LGBTI+ persons, they were generally housed together to insulate against any potential\nharassment. They were reportedly not subjected to any notably different treatment to other detainees or other\ndiscrimination. However, prison officials noted that there were no particular protocols on how to address the\nparticular needs of members of this community in detention. The Working Group considers that the authorities\nshould undertake consultations to determine whether specific protocols are required beyond the existing laws\nand to identify best practices which could be followed.\n3. Implementation and follow-up of the Working Group’s opinions\nDuring the visit, the Working Group met multiple individuals who had been released from detention as a result\nof being subjects of its opinions. This step demonstrates a commitment of the authorities to improving rights\nprotection and compliance with the international human rights norms. However, others whom the Working\nGroup has found to be arbitrarily detained remain in detention. The Working Group calls on the Mexican\nauthorities to systematically implement all opinions which find that arbitrary detention has occurred. The\nGroup further encourages to close gaps in domestic accountability by investigating cases of arbitrary detention\nand paying compensation to the victims, as well as following other recommendations set up in its opinions. It\ncalls on the authorities to conduct public outreach campaigns to raise public awareness of human rights,\nincluding the prohibition of arbitrary detention.\n                                                  Conclusion\nThese are the preliminary findings of the Working Group. We look forward to continue engaging in this\nconstructive dialogue with the Government of Mexico while we determine our final conclusions in relation to\nthis country visit. We acknowledge with gratitude the willingness and openness of the Government to invite\nthe Working Group and note that this is an opportunity for introducing reforms to address situations, which\nmay amount to arbitrary deprivation of liberty.\n\n\n6 United Nations Basic Principles and Guidelines on Remedies and Procedures on the Right of Anyone Deprived of Their Liberty to Bring\n\nProceedings Before a Court, Guideline 20.\n                                                                                                                                        10", "excerpt_sha256": "baf47abc3989e8d05df4d5153b0eb754e06b93d1d4fc91cce3d350aa3fa53220", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt specifically describes child detention as contrary to legal restrictions and migrant detention beyond stated limits; 'unlawful migrant and child detention' was narrowed."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0050-E002", "source_id": "SRC-1d8e95a8a24b4820", "url": "https://www.scielo.org.mx/pdf/ep/n63/0185-1616-ep-63-94.pdf", "title": "Government of AMLO and social policy", "publisher": "SciELO/Estudios Políticos", "raw_sha256": "253733dc10b23d419af935626832cc37c360a6b0d04c8da6e4aeec47f3910d14", "fact_summary": "The government reorganized social policy around universal or progressively universal transfers, created or revised programs, replaced the Social Development Secretariat with the Welfare Secretariat, and delivered benefits directly through cards or cash without intermediaries. However, Coneval evaluations found unclear target populations, weak infrastructure and staffing, insufficient performance in most evaluated actions, and limited measurable impact.", "question_ids": ["6B.1", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.4", "6B.5", "6B.7", "6B.9", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.4", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Describes the AMLO administration, with implementation evidence principally from 2019–2021 and policy continuation into 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The article attributes the policy design and reorganization to López Obrador's government.", "limitations": ["Much of the evidence predates 2023.", "The article synthesizes secondary reporting and does not provide a complete 2023 audit."], "start_unit": 8, "end_unit": 10, "locator": "page 8 through page 10", "exact_excerpt": "bolivar meza r. • gobierno y política social\n\n\n\n\nadultos mayores, personas con discapacidad, estudiantes y población indígena, buscando una\nmayor equidad social, aspirando a restablecer un Estado proveedor descuidado durante el periodo\nneoliberal (Monsiváis-Carrillo, 2019).\n    En su toma de posesión ante el Congreso de la Unión señaló que comenzaba un cambio\nde régimen político y no sólo de gobierno, y que con su arribo al poder terminaban 36 años de\nneoliberalismo. Puso énfasis en señalar que el neoliberalismo, la corrupción y la deshonestidad\nfueron las causantes de la desigualdad económica y social, pero también de la inseguridad y\nla violencia. Delineó sus programas sociales ofrecidos desde la campaña, como las becas para\nestudiantes, aumentar al doble la pensión para adultos mayores y hacerla universal, su programa\nde empleo y capacitación para jóvenes que no estudiaran, así como la creación de universidades\nregionales. En lo económico planteó mantener la autonomía del Banco de México, respetar los\ncontratos suscritos por gobiernos anteriores, garantizar la seguridad de las inversiones nacionales\ny extranjeras, bajar el precio de las gasolinas al terminarse de construir la refinería de Dos Bocas,\nconstruir el Tren Maya, el Tren del Istmo y el aeropuerto internacional de Santa Lucía, llamado\ndespués Aeropuerto Internacional Felipe Ángeles. Planteó bajar el Impuesto al Valor Agregado en\nlos estados del norte del país, aumentar el salario mínimo al doble y reducir el Impuesto Sobre la\nRenta (Cervantes y Gil, 2018).\n    En los primeros cien días de su gobierno desaparecieron o se reajustaron 18 programas sociales.\nPor otra parte, se crearon 14 programas con los que el presidente deseaba eliminar la intermediari-\nzación y garantizar que cada beneficiario recibiera su transferencia sin condiciones. En esta lógica\nestaba el fortalecimiento del mercado interno y permitir que el dinero circulara de mano en mano\nhasta formar un círculo virtuoso de gasto y consumo (Rocha, 2021). De hecho, una de las primeras\nmedidas de este gobierno fue cambiar lo que se conocía como política social por política de bien-\nestar, que aglutinó como un todo la educación, la salud, la seguridad social y la erradicación de la\npobreza. Para ello se sustituyó la Secretaría de Desarrollo Social por la Secretaría de Bienestar, la\ncual atendió fundamentalmente el combate a la pobreza (Curzio y Gutiérrez, 2020).\n    Pasados los primeros cien días de gobierno y en proceso de elaborar el Plan Nacional de\nDesarrollo 2019-2024, López Obrador anunció “el fin de la época neoliberal” en México, por lo\nque se construiría una nueva política posneoliberal, como un proyecto alternativo al existente,\nque se buscaría convertir en un modelo viable de desarrollo económico, ordenamiento político y\nconvivencia social entre todos los sectores sociales, en que el mercado no sustituyera al Estado.\nConsistiría en buscar el crecimiento de la economía junto con la redistribución de la riqueza y del\ningreso (La Jornada, 18 de marzo de 2019, p. 3).\n    En su libro Hacia una economía moral, López Obrador (2019) el presidente definió a la eco-\nnomía moral, como el modelo posneoliberal para México, consistente en construir un nuevo\nordenamiento político y de convivencia, acompañado de un modelo viable de desarrollo eco-\nnómico construido desde abajo y sin dejar fuera a nadie, en que el desarrollo no excluyera a la\njusticia social. Pretendía establecer un Estado de bienestar igualitario y fraterno que garantizara\nque los pobres encontraran protección ante incertidumbres económicas y desigualdades sociales.\nTuvo como ideal proteger a todas las personas a lo largo de su vida, garantizando su derecho a la\nalimentación, al trabajo, la salud, la educación y la cultura, la vivienda y la seguridad social.\n\n\n\n\nestudios políticos, novena época, núm. 63 (septiembre-diciembre, 2024): 94-109                              101\n\nbolivar meza r. • gobierno y política social\n\n\n\n\nResultados de la aplicación de la política social\n\nPara el Coneval, en un inicio los programas sociales del gobierno de López Obrador fueron pues-\ntos en práctica con deficiencias en su diseño. Luego de presentar el resultado de la evaluación a 17\nprogramas sociales en su primer año de ejecución, a través de trabajo de campo y con un enfoque\nparticipativo, consideró que carecieron de una definición clara de su población objetivo y de los\nproblemas sociales que buscaban atender. Por el contexto de austeridad en que se desarrollaron,\noperaron con muy poca infraestructura física y recursos humanos, y no se logró medir un impacto\nclaro de los programas en las condiciones de vida de la población a los que estaban dirigidos.\nEntre los programas evaluados se encontraban Apoyos para el Bienestar de las Niñas y los Niños\nHijos de Madres Trabajadoras, Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro, Sembrando Vida, Pensión para el\nBienestar de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente, Precios de Garantía a Productos Alimen-\ntarios Básicos, Crédito Ganadero a la Palabra, así como el de Fertilizantes (Reforma, 7 de julio de\n2020, p. 2).\n    Con base en las Consideraciones para el Proceso Presupuestario 2021, elaboradas por el Co-\nneval, se consideró que todavía durante 2019 los programas sociales tuvieron un desempeño\ninsuficiente, ya que de 20 acciones que en conjunto sumaron 898 mil millones de pesos, en ocho\nde ellas el desempeño fue apropiado y en el resto no fue suficiente, por lo que consideró funda-\nmental que la asignación presupuestal tomara en cuenta el desempeño de los programas sociales,\nlo cual se midió a partir del cumplimiento de las metas de los indicadores de cada acción que\nse estableció al inicio de cada año. Entre los programas que de acuerdo con el Coneval tuvieron\nun desempeño apropiado, estuvieron el de la pensión a los adultos mayores, IMSS Bienestar y el\nde becas de posgrado del entonces Consejo Nacional de Ciencia y Tecnología. Evaluados como\ninsuficientes fueron las becas en otros niveles educativos, sembrando vida, la pensión para la po-\nblación con discapacidad permanente, atención a la salud, producción para el bienestar y precios\nde garantía, suministro de medicamentos del ISSSTE, así como servicios de guardería y atención a\nla Salud del IMSS (La Jornada, 15 de agosto de 2020, p. 11).\n    También con base en datos del Coneval de agosto de 2021, pero haciendo un corte al 31\nde diciembre de 2020, en gran medida por los efectos de la pandemia de Covid-19 la pobreza\nmoderada y la pobreza extrema aumentaron en México. Los programas sociales del gobierno lo\nque hicieron fue atenuar el fenómeno y evitar que el nivel del rezago aumentara más. Con base\nen la información reportada, la pobreza aumentó de 41.9%, con 51.9 millones de personas, a\n43.9%, con 55.7 millones y de no haber sido por estos programas sociales, el porcentaje podría\nhaber ascendido al 45.9% de la población, es decir, a 58.2 millones de habitantes (La Jornada, 6\nde agosto de 2021, p. 9).\n    Pese a todos los esfuerzos hechos en materia de política social, en los inicios del gobierno\nde la 4T aún faltaba mucho por hacer para estar dentro de los estándares internacionales, ya que\ncon base en datos de la Organización para la Cooperación y el Desarrollo Económico (OCDE),\nhasta 2019 México seguía siendo el país con menor gasto social de todos los que integraban esta\norganización, estando incluso por debajo de otros países de América Latina como Chile, Colombia\ny Costa Rica, ya que el gasto público que destinaba a pensiones, servicios de salud, sistemas de\ncuidado a infantes, apoyo a desempleados y para educación, entre otros, apenas alcanzó el 7.5%\ndel Producto Interno Bruto (PIB), que era menos de la mitad del promedio de 20% que tenían los\npaíses de la OCDE, pues por ejemplo, Francia, Austria, Bélgica, Dinamarca, Finlandia, Alemania,\n\n\n\n102                               estudios políticos, novena época, núm. 63 (septiembre-diciembre, 2024): 94-109\n\nbolivar meza r. • gobierno y política social\n\n\n\n\nItalia, Noruega y Suecia invertían más de la cuarta parte de su PIB en servicios y transferencias para\nla población (La Jornada, 27 de noviembre de 2020, p. 30).6\n     Pese a esto, los programas prioritarios pretendían resolver problemas sociales estructurales,\npero arrancaron con premura y con carencias de diseño, que dificultaron su operación. Inclusive\nalgunos de ellos tuvieron inicialmente poca eficacia por la política de austeridad, los recortes pre-\nsupuestarios y también repercusiones negativas por la pandemia generada por el virus de Covid-19.\n     Debido a la pérdida de ingresos y fuentes de trabajo para millones de personas por la crisis\neconómica asociada al Covid-19, los programas sociales fueron insuficientes para paliar los efectos\ndel empobrecimiento (Gómez, 2020). Ante ello, el presidente López Obrador optó por radicalizar\nsus políticas antineoliberales al no endeudarse, no hacer un rescate generalizado de los empre-\nsarios (sólo ofrecer créditos a los micro y pequeños empresarios), concentrando su atención en\ngarantizar el financiamiento de sus programas sociales mediante un decreto de mayor austeridad\ny reasignación de fondos, entre ellos eliminar algunos fideicomisos. Ante la gravedad de esta\npandemia, el presidente decidió mantener intactos los programas sociales de su gobierno, con la\nintención de con ellos compensar los daños provocados por la recesión económica, desempleo\nmasivo y reducción de los ingresos familiares (Valdés, 2020).\n     La entrega de los recursos a los beneficiarios de los programas sociales se hizo directamente\nmediante transferencias a tarjetas bancarias o dispersión de recursos en efectivo, sin pasar por in-\ntermediarios. Esta estrategia social la operaron la Secretaría de Bienestar, que controlaba el padrón\nde derechohabientes de la 4T, llamado Censo del Bienestar, teniendo a su servicio para operar\nprácticamente todos los programas sociales a los casi 20 mil “servidores de la nación”, así como la\nCoordinación General de Programas para el Desarrollo, adscrita a la Presidencia de la República,\nla cual controlaba a los delegados del bienestar en los estados (Tourliere, 2020).\n     A diferencia de los programas sociales de gobiernos anteriores, que eran focalizados a ciertos\nsectores de la población y que por lo tanto podían ser manejados en forma clientelar, los del\ngobierno de López Obrador tuvieron una tendencia progresiva a la universalización, en la que se\nbeneficiaran todas las personas a las que iban dirigidos y con una política social con enfoque de de-\nrechos sociales para todos sus beneficiarios. Es por ello que la política social del gobierno tuvo una\npreocupación genuina por disminuir la desigualdad. Intentó afianzar su base social favoreciendo a\nla población que no podía satisfacer sus necesidades básicas de alimentación y salud, acceder a una\nmejor educación o capacitarse para un mejor empleo. De ahí que buscara la institucionalización\nde la política social, garantizando que los programas sociales se aplicaran independientemente de\nquién gobernara (Illades, 2020), quedando inscritos en el artículo 4 constitucional.\n     Siguiendo con la evaluación de la política social del gobierno de López Obrador, con base en\nla Encuesta Nacional de Ingresos y Gastos de los Hogares, correspondiente a 2022, se observó que\nMéxico era ya menos desigual que en 2018, según lo demostraron diversos indicadores como el\n\n\n\n6\n    Los críticos del gobierno de López Obrador consideraron cuestionable la creencia de que los programas sociales\n    impulsados por la 4T representaban un cambio paradigmático para la política social, pues argumentaron que se\n    mantenían las características de focalización y condicionalidad de los programas sociales de los gobiernos anteriores.\n    Cuestionaron que los nuevos programas sociales no contaran con reglas de operación y que no se hiciera público su\n    padrón de beneficiarios. Otro aspecto a destacar fue que la desigualdad\nentre 2018 y 2022 no sólo disminuyó entre hogares y entre personas, sino también entre entidades\nfederativas, pues el ingreso promedio en estados pobres como Chiapas, tendió a crecer más rápido\nque el de hogares de entidades ricas, como la Ciudad de México (Esquivel, 2023).\n    Con base en los resultados de la Medición Multidimensional de la Pobreza 2022 del Coneval\n(entre 2020 y 2022), la cifra de personas en situación de pobreza en México se redujo en 8.9\nmillones. Para 2020 había 55.7 millones de mexicanos en esa condición, mientras que para 2022\nera de 46.8 millones, con lo que el porcentaje de población con grandes carencias sociales pasó\nde 43.9 a 36.3%. De igual manera, la pobreza extrema también disminuyó al pasar del 8.5 al 7.1%,\ncon lo que dejaron de estar en esa clasificación 1.7 millones de personas, ya que mientras que en\n2020 eran 10.8 millones, en 2022 fueron 9.1 millones.\n    Lo que influyó en esto fueron el incremento al salario mínimo y a los ingresos laborales, los\nprogramas sociales del gobierno federal y las remesas. Estos aspectos positivos influyeron también\nen la población no pobre ni vulnerable (la que podría ubicarse como clase media), ya que se\nincrementó en 3.6%, al pasar de 23.5% a 27.1%, con lo que en estos dos años de referencia 5.1\nmillones de personas mejoraron sus ingresos.\n    No obstante a que disminuyó la cantidad de pobres, el Coneval señaló que hubo un alza en\ndos carencias: el acceso a la salud y a la seguridad social. En el primer caso fue de 16.2% a 39.1%\nentre 2018 y 2022, es decir, pasaron de 20.1 a 50.4 millones de personas. En el segundo caso, en\nlo que respecta al rezago educativo, en esos mismos cuatro años, se pasó de 19% a 19.4%, lo que\nequivalía a 23.5 millones de personas en 2018 a 25.1 millones en 2022, es decir, 1.6 millones de\npersonas más (La Jornada, 11 de agosto de 2023, p. 3).\n    El gobierno de López Obrador triplicó durante su administración el presupuesto de los progra-\nmas sociales. Para 2024, en los 12 principales programas sociales se presupuestó un gasto de 680\nmil millones de pesos que beneficiarían a 14.6 millones de mexicanos, mientras que en el primer\naño de gobierno se destinaron 227 mil millones de pesos. Esto propició que algunos programas\nsociales tuvieran aumentos desde el 100% hasta casi 400% en comparación con los recursos\neconómicos gastados a inicios del gobierno. De todos los programas sociales, el que más se\nincrementó fue el de la Pensión del Bienestar para Adultos Mayores, al pasar de 100 mil millones\nde pesos en 2019, a un presupuesto programado de 465 mil millones de pesos, lo que representó\nun alza sexenal de 365%.\n    Con base en datos de la Secretaría de Bienestar, en 2020 había nueve millones de adultos\nmayores beneficiados con la pensión del Bienestar, quienes recibían cada mes mil 292 pesos. Para\nfines de 2023 su número ascendió a 11.8 millones de derechohabientes con un ingreso mensual\nde 2 mil 400 pesos, e iniciando 2024 recibieron ya tres mil pesos mensuales. A partir de estos\nnúmeros, el Coneval señaló que con este programa el nivel de pobreza de la población mayor de\n65 años disminuyó 12% con respecto a la medición de 2018. If a patient can’t get needed medications at a local hospital, the patient, the patient’s doctor or the pharmacist would be able to call up the warehouse and get it delivered from the huge 40,000 square meter (430,000 square foot) Mexico City warehouse.\nThe armed forces, or the government-run pharmaceutical company Birmex, will ship the drugs out by land or air “within 24 to 48 hours,” López Obrador pledged.\nThe question is whether Mexico can overcome its history of being bad at regulating the pharmaceutical industry, bad at buying medicines, bad at storing them, and bad at distributing them. Extreme centralization also hasn’t helped Mexico much in the past in many areas.\nThe most visible face of this problem are the parents of children with cancer, who frequently stage protests because they say that in recent years chemotherapy and other drugs have been impossible to obtain.\n\nRelated Stories\nCuba loosens control of its private sector in a struggle with a mounting humanitarian crisis\n3 MIN READ\n13\n‘She killed the kids!’ 911 call from Lindsay Clancy’s husband brings tears to the courtroom\n4 MIN READ\n176\nFederal survey shows promising trends for youth mental health and drug use\n1 MIN READ\nDesperate parents blocked traffic at the Mexico City airport last year, holding up a banner reading: “There isn’t any chemotherapy, treatment or medicines, have some empathy and sensitivity.”\nRead More\nThe problems have killed otherwise healthy people. Because Mexico has had problems in obtaining enough morphine, anesthesiologists in Mexico have had to carry around their own vials of the sedative, drawing multiple doses out of a single vial for routine procedures like spinal blocks during births.\nIn the United States, where there is no shortage of morphine, doctors are advised to draw a single dose from a vial and throw the remainder out.\nBut in Mexico,\nthat has led to contamination of the vials,\ntriggering\noutbreaks of injection-induced meningitis in two Mexican states that have killed dozens of people\n— including some Americans who sought treatment at clinics in the border city of Matamoros, across from Brownsville, Texas.\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\n\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nLópez Obrador mounted a major effort to obtain COVID-19 vaccines in 2021, using the armed forces to distribute them and volunteers to help apply them, and by the end of that year just about anybody in Mexico who wanted a vaccine got one, for free.\nBut trying to replicate that model of centralized government purchasing and army distribution on a national scale for thousands of medications is not the same, according to Mauricio Rodríguez, a professor at the School of Medicine at Mexico’s National Autonomous University.\n“This is crazy,” said Rodríguez, noting the government is opening the centralized warehouse without answering how the system will operate, especially for urgently-needed medications. He noted that concentrating all the drugs at one site increases risks, and could sideline some already-existing distribution systems.\nMany of the problems pre-date López Obrador, who took office in late 2018. For decades, there have been scandals involving of millions of dollars worth of medicines going out-of-date at warehouses while hospitals couldn’t get them.\nThe country’s medicine regulatory agency, known by its Spanish acronym as\nCofepris, was already so riddled with corruption\nprior to López Obrador that regulators would hide applications for approval of new medicines for years, and demand bribes to approve them.\nAnd with alarming frequency, regulators in Mexico send out alerts about falsified or knock-off medications being sold for treating everything from cancer to heart disease. Boxes, labels, vials and certifications are copied with amazing accuracy, but the bottles often contain little or none of the medication.\nThe fake medicine trade is so common, and so lucrative in Mexico\nbecause patients or their relatives are often told by doctors to buy medications at private drug stores when they are unavailable at government hospitals.\nThe civic group “Zero Shortages” said there was an increase of 142% in the number of alerts about falsified medicines between 2021 and 2022.\nBut part of the problems are of López Obrador’s own making. Angry at what he claimed were inflated profits made by drug distributors and importers, the president simply cut the private companies out and decided the government should directly buy all medications.\nBecause the government did not have much infrastructure, contacts or experience in such a massive effort, López Obrador signed an agreement with the World Health Organization to help Mexico in purchasing. But even with that help, Mexico was unable to obtain some specialized medication, something López Obrador blamed on sabotage by pharmaceutical firms.\nRafael Gual, the director of the National Pharmaceutical Industry Chamber, said the government’s own actions created “bottlenecks” in distribution.\nAccording to the group “Zero Shortages,” the number of prescriptions that went unfilled in Mexico rose from 1.5 million in 2019 to 22 million in 2021; disruptions due to the COVID pandemic probably played a role.\nBut even in 2022, there were still about 12.5 million prescriptions that went unfilled.\nDr. José Moya, WHO’s Mexico representative, said centralized medical warehouses can be a solution, but the key is to have a good logistical system.\n“If they are considering a warehouse like this, it’s because there is a need,” Moya said, “and this has to be very well organized.”\nMARÍA VERZA\nVerza has focused on immigration, violence and human rights stories in Mexico and Central America for more than a decade\ntwitter\ninstagram\nmailto", "excerpt_sha256": "08c0072bdefb71e6245d121877e6cc0caa134ef36bce56093ab9e9c9bd8a816d", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not explicitly state December 29, 2023; The Category 5 hurricane damaged nearly all of Acapulco’s homes, left bodies bobbing along the coastline and much of the city foraging for food.\nWhile authorities were hard at work restoring order in Acapulco’s tourist center — cutting through trees in front of high-rise hotels and restoring power — the city’s poorest, like Sandoval, said they felt abandoned.\nShe and hundreds of thousands others lived two hours of terror last week\n, and now face years of work to repair their already precarious lives.\nRelated Stories\nTropical Storm Fausto to splash past Hawaii as Hurricane Genevieve sends swells to Mexico\n1 MIN READ\nMajor Hurricane Genevieve roars in the Pacific as Fausto weakens to a tropical storm near Hawaii\n1 MIN READ\n14\nGenevieve becomes a major Pacific hurricane off Mexico, but no direct threat to land is forecast\n1 MIN READ\n“The government doesn’t even know we exist,” Sandoval said. “They’ve only ever taken care of the resort areas, the pretty places of Acapulco. the date was removed."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0050-E013", "source_id": "SRC-2f8b405bc92b4aae", "url": "https://www.bakerinstitute.org/research/mexico-country-outlook-2023", "title": "Mexico Country Outlook 2023", "publisher": "Baker Institute for Public Policy", "raw_sha256": "76b372b35c2b15786c0d1c10fb0e39c27c2aaea47b6fe2a19425ba89afba25d7", "fact_summary": "The Baker Institute forecast that in 2023 López Obrador would continue expanding military responsibilities, face pressure from major projects and social programs, and pursue policies that critics viewed as weakening electoral institutions, regulatory independence, health, education, and the investment climate; it also described continued USMCA and energy disputes.", "question_ids": ["4B.2", "4B.4", "4B.9", "5B.3", "5B.4", "5B.6", "8B.1", "8B.2"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "A January 2023 forecast and assessment of the expected year.", "ruler_attribution": "The report directly attributes the forecast policy direction to López Obrador and Morena.", "limitations": ["This is a forecast, not a retrospective factual account of all 2023 outcomes.", "The institute's assessments include interpretation and prediction."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 7, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 7", "exact_excerpt": "Linkedin\nDownload PDF\nPrint This Publication\nCite This Publication\nTony Payan, \"Mexico Country Outlook 2023\" (Houston: Rice University’s Baker Institute for Public Policy, January 9, 2023).\nCopy Citation\nTags\nMexico\nDrug cartels\nEducation\nEconomic growth\nEmployment\nHealth care\nThis report was written collaboratively by experts from the Baker Institute\nCenter for the U.S. and Mexico\nand other contributors.\nLearn more\nabout the annual Mexico Country Outlook conference and accompanying report.\nExecutive Summary\nThe Mexico Country Outlook examines how key issues driving Mexico’s politics, economy, border policies and more will likely play out in 2023. The report is designed to help businesses and investors make decisions related to their interests in Mexico and to help policymakers understand what they can expect to see next year. Drawing on their decades of study and analysis of Mexico, the experts of the Center for the U.S. and Mexico conclude that in 2023:\nElections in the State of Mexico and Coahuila will be a litmus test for political coalitions and parties heading into the 2024 presidential election.\nMexico’s House of Representatives will appoint four new commissioners to the powerful National Electoral Institute (INE), giving President Andrés Manuel López Obrador a new opportunity to coopt this key organization.\nLópez Obrador will continue to give the military responsibilities traditionally carried out by civilians.\nMexico’s Supreme Court justices will elect a new chief justice from their ranks, setting up a more collaborative—or a more adversarial—relationship with the executive branch.\nMexico’s economy is expected to grow between 0.5% and 1.3%, which means the country’s GDP will not return to 2018 levels until 2024.\n\nThe financial demands of the president’s pet projects and social programs, and the poor prospects for economic growth, will further pressure the federal budget.\nInflation will come down slowly, with headline inflation reaching around 5% and core inflation 4.9% in 2023.\nThe U.S. dollar-Mexican peso exchange rate will stand at around 21.1 on average. However, if the U.S. goes into recession, the peso may lose value well beyond that.\nFederal funds for health care will not be enough to compensate for Mexico’s failing health infrastructure, and the fallout will affect millions.\nLow levels investment in education will continue. Electoral tensions caused by powerful teachers’ unions will affect the education agenda.\nViolent conflicts between organized crime groups will likely escalate as they try to influence politics and get a firmer foothold in licit markets.\nThe blatant use of regulatory tools to shield PEMEX and CFE market positions will not wane, but energy self-sufficiency will continue to be an elusive goal.\nThe Biden administration may seek to designate a new ambassador to Mexico.\nU.S. will continue to pressure Mexico on the full implementation of the USMCA, immigration, and drug smuggling, particularly fentanyl.\nMexico will likely accept more migrants from Cuba, Nicaragua, and Venezuela under the U.S. “Remain in Mexico Program.” Thousands of migrants will continue to amass at the U.S.-Mexico border.\nA water governance crisis in the river basins shared by Mexico and the United States will continue amid abnormally dry conditions.\nI. Introduction\nThe U.S.-Mexico relationship is multi-faceted, with numerous issues and interests running through it. Despite expectations of a rough road when President Andrés Manuel López Obrador took office in 2018, the first four years of his administration did not produce a major crisis with the United States. Mexico’s nationalistic administration was a surprisingly good match for the Trump administration’s interests, despite Trump’s controversial stands on the North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA), immigration, and Mexico in general. Binational relations were tested, but not derailed. The Biden administration replaced Trump’s insults and threats with a focused and disciplined form of diplomacy. Even as its attention is diverted by other major foreign and domestic problems, the Biden White House keeps a close eye on Mexico’s myriad domestic issues, including its assault on democracy; problems with the rule of law; deterioration of human rights; increased militarization of the country’s public life; inaction on human smuggling and drug trafficking, particularly fentanyl; growth of organized crime and corruption; and Mexico City’s deference toward autocratic regimes in Latin American and cozier-than-warranted position toward Russia in particular. None of these issues will produce a major showdown in 2023, but there will be disagreements that will keep the U.S. and Mexico governments talking throughout the year.\nThe continued conversation especially applies to the countries’ economic ties, the brightest spot of the relationship. Cooperation will likely continue on its current path, though hampered by ongoing disputes about energy sector policies, which particularly interest López Obrador. The disagreements over the United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement’s (USMCA) coverage of the energy sector will continue well into 2023 and represent a relatively important and active obstacle to broader cooperation on other issues. Still, the economic, and especially the commercial, relationship will continue to be robust and grow on the basis of the USMCA framework. Mexico’s stance toward the U.S., moderated by continued dependence on access to U.S. markets and investment, will aim to avoid a major crisis with Washington. Similarly, Washington’s preoccupation with an increasingly complicated international scene and domestic political turmoil driving toward 2024 will lead it to avoid a diplomatic escalation with Mexico City. This situation means there will be no major breakthroughs on the strained status quo.\nThe turmoil expected in Mexico toward the end of 2023, when the country enters into a full transition toward the presidential race in summer 2024, will draw Washington’s attention. Mexico’s anti-Washington rhetoric is expected to play out during the country’s election season, though the White House will likely respond conservatively, holding its basic line on disputed issues.\nThe following sections of this report break down by topic Mexico’s outlook for 2023 and their impact on the binational relationship throughout the year.\nII. A Shifting Political Landscape\nHigh-stakes 2023 Elections\nOn June 4, 2023, the State of Mexico—the largest in the country—and the State of Coahuila will hold elections for governor and, in the case of the latter, for state legislators and mayors. The Institutional Revolutionary Party (PRI) has continuously governed both states since the 1920s, but the PRI’s long run will probably end if a coalition is not formed to run against MORENA.\nThe 2023 elections will test coalitions, parties, and candidates heading into the 2024 presidential elections.
